1:uf|## opencascade-sys API Patterns
2:dd|
3:lz|The Rust bindings mirror OCCT C++ closely but with automatic name transformations:
4:dd|
5:hx|### Module and Type Naming
6:fs|- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeEdge` → `b_rep_builder_api::MakeEdge`
7:pg|- `BRep_Tool` → `b_rep::Tool`
8:un|- `gp_Pnt` → `gp::Pnt`
9:dq|- `TopExp_Explorer` → `top_exp::Explorer`
10:tl|- `GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnSurf` → `geom_api::ProjectPointOnSurf`
11:fj|- `STEPControl_Reader` → `step_control::Reader`
12:qf|- `RWStl` → `rw_stl` (free functions)
13:dd|
14:jh|### Constructors
15:me|Named `::new_<compressed_param_types>()`. For example:
16:fj|```rust
17:gp|gp::Pnt::new_real3(x, y, z)           // gp_Pnt(double, double, double)
18:vv|gp::Dir::new_real3(dx, dy, dz)
19:nc|top_exp::Explorer::new_shape_shapeenum2(shape, from_type, avoid_type)
20:wj|geom_api::ProjectPointOnSurf::new_pnt_handlegeomsurface_extalgo(pt, surf, algo)
21:pi|```
22:dd|
23:ds|### Ownership
24:cw|- All objects are returned as `OwnedPtr<T>` which auto-derefs via `Deref`/`DerefMut`
25:dk|- `OwnedPtr<T>` is essentially `Box<T>` for OCCT heap objects
26:dd|
27:bo|### Handles (OCCT `Handle<T>`)
28:xj|- `Type::to_handle(owned_ptr)` consumes `OwnedPtr<T>` → `OwnedPtr<HandleT>`
29:rh|- `.get()` dereferences a handle to access the underlying object
30:sv|- Upcasting: `.to_handle_surface()`, `.to_handle_curve()`, etc.
31:mo|- Downcasting: `.downcast_to_plane()` returns `Option<OwnedPtr<HandleDerived>>`
32:dd|
33:uk|### Enums
34:bv|Typed Rust enums, not raw integers:
35:fj|```rust
36:wl|top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face
37:xr|extrema::ExtAlgo::Grad
38:pi|```
39:dd|
40:oc|### Shape Downcasting
41:pu|`topo_ds::face_shape` is a safe function, but it panics if the shape is not a Face. Always guard with `shape_type()`:
42:fj|```rust
43:qu|if shape_ref.shape_type() == top_abs::ShapeEnum::Face {
44:nm|    let face = topo_ds::face_shape(shape_ref);  // &topo_ds::Shape → &topo_ds::Face
45:bf|    ...
46:io|}
47:pi|```
48:dd|
49:ej|### Static Methods → Free Functions
50:qz|OCCT utility classes (e.g., `BRep_Tool`) become module-level free functions or associated functions on the type.
51:dd|
52:va|## Common API Recipes
53:dd|
54:tt|### Read an STL file → vertex positions
55:fj|```rust
56:xj|use opencascade_sys::{rw_stl, message};
57:nr|let progress = message::ProgressRange::new();
58:ci|let tri_handle = rw_stl::read_file_charptr_progressrange_2(path, &progress);
59:kc|let tri = tri_handle.get();  // &Poly_Triangulation
60:xd|for i in 1..=tri.nb_nodes() {
61:rn|    let pt = tri.node(i);  // OwnedPtr<gp::Pnt>
62:xp|    let (x, y, z) = (pt.x(), pt.y(), pt.z());
63:io|}
64:pi|```
65:dd|
66:zd|### Read a STEP file → TopoDS_Shape
67:fj|```rust
68:sg|use opencascade_sys::{step_control, message};
69:dl|let mut reader = step_control::Reader::new();
70:bz|reader.read_file_charptr(path);
71:zy|reader.transfer_roots(&message::ProgressRange::new());
72:fa|let shape = reader.one_shape();  // OwnedPtr<topo_ds::Shape>
73:pi|```
74:dd|
75:br|### Iterate faces of a shape
76:fj|```rust

83:rn|    // ... use face
84:zk|    explorer.next();
85:io|}
86:pi|```

# Brepper - STL to STEP Converter

Convert triangulated STL meshes from CAD exports into parametric STEP files with fitted analytic and freeform surfaces.

## Usage

```
brepper - Convert STL mesh to STEP with fitted surfaces

USAGE:
    brepper [OPTIONS] <input.stl> [-o <output.step>]

REQUIRED:
    <input.stl>              Input STL file (binary or ASCII)

GENERAL OPTIONS:
    -o <step>, --output=<step>  Output STEP file
    --stl-units=<units>      Units used by the STL file (mm, cm, m, in, ft, um; default: mm)
    --step-units=<units>     Units to use in exported STEP file (default: mm)
    --compare=<step>         STEP file to compare to at each step
    --vertex-tolerance=<value>  Fitting tolerance in STL units (default: 1e-5)
    --surface-tolerance=<value> Surface-to-face offset tolerance in STL units (default: 0.4)
    --angular-tolerance=<deg>   Max dihedral angle between adjacent triangles on the same surface (default: 17.5)
    -v, --verbose[=LEVEL]    Verbosity level: 1 (or bare -v) = summaries, -vv or --verbose=2 = per-face details, -vvv or --verbose=3 = full BFS trace
    -q, --quiet              Suppress non-error output
    --debug                  Enable debug output and intermediate files
    --stage=<stage>          Stop after stage, e.g. 2.2 (default: 4.1). --stage=2 stops after all of stage2
    --viz-stages=<stages>    Open interactive 3D visualization at specified stages (e.g. 2.1,2.2,2.3)
```

### Examples

```bash
# Full conversion
./target/debug/brepper input.stl -o output.step

# Just load and validate mesh (stage 1.2)
./target/debug/brepper input.stl --stage 1.2 -v

# Compare mesh against reference STEP at each stage
./target/debug/brepper input.stl --compare reference.step --stage 1 -v
```

See [DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md](DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md) for full documentation.

### Tolerance Settings

#### Surface Tolerance

This is how far the surface of a mesh triangle is allowed to deviate from the surface it is approximating.

Onshape calls this "Chordal tolerance (mm)", and the preset values are:
- Coarse: 0.24 mm
- Medium: 0.12 mm
- Fine: 0.06 mm

Fusion 360 calls this "Surface Deviation", and the preset values seem to vary based on model size:
- Low: 1x
- Medium: 2.5x
- High: 8x

#### Angular Tolerance

This is the maximum angle between adjacent triangle faces that approximate a smooth surface.

Onshape calls this "Angular deviation (deg)", and the present values are:
- Coarse: 12.5 deg
- Medium: 6.25 deg
- Fine: 2.5 deg

Fusion 360 calls this "Normal Deviation", and the preset values are:
- Low: 30.0
- Medium: 15.0
- High: 10.0
In theory "Normal Deviation" should be the maximum angular error of the face relative to the surface normal, but in practice it seems to result in the given maximum angular deviation.

### Generating Test Models

Some tests use models generated via [CodeCAD](https://codecad.xyz/). The generated STL/STEP files are committed to the repo, so CodeCAD is not required to run tests.

To add or modify these models:
1. Install CodeCAD
2. Edit files in `tests/ccad/parts/` — see `tests/ccad/README.md` for primitive conventions and placement rules
3. Run `./tests/ccad/generate_models.sh`

**Important:** `box(w,d,h)` places a corner at the origin, not centered. Use `center_xyz()` to center. Each part file includes analytical surface area and volume in its header comments.


## Viewer

`viewer` is an interactive 3D viewer for STL and STEP files.

```
USAGE:
    viewer [OPTIONS] <file>...

ARGUMENTS:
    <file>...   One or more STL or STEP files to view (detected by extension)

OPTIONS:
    --deflection <value>          Linear deflection for STEP tessellation (default: 0.1)
    --angular-deflection <value>  Angular deflection for STEP tessellation in radians (default: 0.5)
```

Multiple files can be loaded simultaneously; each is rendered in a distinct color.

### Examples

```bash
# View a single STEP file
./target/release/viewer part.step

# View a STEP and an STL side by side
./target/release/v
... (truncated, 9724 chars)
````

# STL to STEP Conversion Utility - Development Plan

## Project Overview

**brepper** (B-Rep from Mesh) - A utility to convert triangulated STL meshes from CAD exports into parametric STEP files with fitted analytic and freeform surfaces.

## Assumptions

- Assumes the vertices of the mesh lie precisely on the CAD surfaces, modulo numerical error/stability.
- Assumes transitions between shapes always have a seam.
- Assumes manifold mesh (each edge shared by exactly 2 faces).
- Assumes consistent face orientation (all normals point outward).

Fails noisily if any of the assumptions are violated. Possible future work: offer a repair mode.

## Dependencies

| Library | Purpose | Version |
|---------|---------|---------|
| OpenCASCADE (OCCT) | STL import, B-Rep modeling, surface fitting, STEP export | latest |

Use the opencascade binding in ../opencascade-rs/crates/opencascade-sys, including porting instructions in PORTING.md and details on the FFI mapping in ../opencascade-rs/crates/opencascade-binding-generator/README.md. It's a reasonably complete binding of the C++ API of the opencascade library which resides in ../opencascade-rs/crates/occt-sys/OCCT, with source code in src/ and documentation in dox/.

### Required OCCT Functionality

This section enumerates the OCCT classes and functions needed, for evaluating Rust binding coverage.

**Stage 1: Mesh Input**
- `RWStl` / `RWStl_Reader` — Read binary/ASCII STL files
- `gp_Pnt` — 3D point representation
- `gp_Dir` / `gp_Vec` — Direction and vector types

**Stage 2: Surface Fitting**
- `gp_Pln` — Plane geometry (normal + distance representation)
- `gp_Cylinder` / `gp_Ax3` — Cylindrical surface geometry (axis + radius)
- `gp_Sphere` — Spherical surface geometry (center + radius)
- `gp_Cone` — Conical surface geometry (axis + half-angle)
- `gp_Torus` — Toroidal surface geometry (for fillets)

**Stage 3: Surface Reconstruction**

*Geometry (infinite surfaces and curves):*
- `Geom_Plane`, `Geom_CylindricalSurface`, `Geom_SphericalSurface`, `Geom_ConicalSurface`, `Geom_ToroidalSurface` — Analytic surface objects
- `Geom_BSplineSurface` — NURBS/B-spline surfaces
- `Geom_Line`, `Geom_Circle`, `Geom_Ellipse` — Analytic curve types
- `Geom_BSplineCurve` — Freeform curves
- `Geom2d_Curve`, `Geom2d_Line`, `Geom2d_Circle`, `Geom2d_BSplineCurve` — 2D curves for pcurves

*Surface fitting:*
- `GeomAPI_PointsToBSplineSurface` — Fit B-spline surface to points

*Surface-surface intersection:*
- `GeomAPI_IntSS` — Compute intersection curves between two surfaces

*Point-curve projection:*
- `GeomAPI_ProjectPointOnCurve` — Project point onto curve (for trimming)
- `ShapeAnalysis_Curve` — Curve analysis utilities

*Topology (B-Rep construction):*
- `TopoDS_Vertex`, `TopoDS_Edge`, `TopoDS_Wire`, `TopoDS_Face`, `TopoDS_Shell`, `TopoDS_Solid`, `TopoDS_Compound` — Topological entities
- `BRep_Builder` — Low-level topology construction
- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeVertex` — Create vertices
- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeEdge` — Create edges from curves (with optional pcurves)
- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeWire` — Assemble edges into wires
- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeFace` — Create faces from surface + bounding wires
- `BRepBuilderAPI_Sewing` — Stitch faces into shells
- `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeSolid` — Create solids from shells

*Topology exploration:*
- `TopExp_Explorer` — Iterate over sub-shapes
- `TopExp::MapShapes` — Build maps of topology
- `BRep_Tool` — Extract geometry from topology (IsSeam, SameParameter, etc.)

*Shape healing and fixing:*
- `ShapeFix_Shape` — General shape repair
- `ShapeFix_Face` — Face-specific repairs
- `ShapeFix_Wire` — Wire repairs, including `FixEdgeCurves` for pcurve computation
- `ShapeFix_Shell` — Shell orientation fixing
- `ShapeFix_Solid` — Solid creation/repair, `SolidFromShell`

*Shape analysis and validation:*
- `BRepCheck_Analyzer` — Validate B-Rep topology/geometry
- `ShapeAnalysis_Shell` — Shell analysis (e.g., `CheckOrientedShells`)
- `GProp_GProps` / `BRepGProp` — Compute volume, area, center o
... (truncated, 87313 chars)
```

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 436-470 of <a href="../DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md#L436-L471">DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md</a></summary>

```
- With the `--compare` flag, for each selected surface:
    - Compute face centroids and project them onto the selected surface.
    - Measure distance from each projected centroid to the nearest surface in the reference STEP file.
    - For all hypotheses, report an error if distance exceeds `--surface-tolerance`.

#### 2.7 Surface refitting (optional)

After stage 2.6 selects surfaces, some border faces may have been absorbed by a hypothesis that fits within tolerance but isn't the best fit. This step refines assignments and re-fits surfaces to improve accuracy.

**Algorithm:**
1. For each mesh face, check whether it belongs to more than one hypothesis (i.e., it has valid assignments in multiple hypothesis types — planar, cylindrical, spherical).
2. For each such face, compute the vertex-to-surface distance for each candidate surface (using the selected surfaces from 2.6, not the raw hypotheses).
3. Reassign the face to the selected surface with the smallest maximum vertex distance.
4. After all reassignments, re-fit each affected surface from its updated face/vertex set to remove any bias introduced by the extra vertices.

This step is expected to be most useful at boundaries between surfaces of different types (e.g., where a cylinder meets a plane), where BFS may have greedily absorbed a border face that technically fits within tolerance but belongs more naturally to the adjacent surface. It may be unnecessary if the greedy area-based selection in 2.6 already produces clean boundaries — implement only if stage 3 reconstruction reveals boundary accuracy problems.

---

### Stage 3: Surface Reconstruction

Build OCCT B-Rep topology (faces, edges, vertices) from the selected surfaces. This is the most complex stage — surface intersections in 3D are numerically delicate, and small perturbations can cause large changes in intersection curves or cause them to disappear.

**Core data structures:**

A vector of `FaceDescriptor` structs, one per selected surface:
- `selected_surface_idx: usize` — Index into the selected surfaces from stage 2.6.
- `surface: OwnedPtr<HandleGeomSurface>` — Infinite OCCT surface, type-erased (created in 3.1).
- `adjacent_faces: Vec<usize>` — Indices of adjacent FaceDescriptors, ordered topologically around this face's boundary. A face may appear multiple times if it shares multiple edges with this face (e.g., a cylindrical face adjacent to itself via a seam edge).
- `edge_indices: Vec<usize>` — ReconEdge indices. `edge_indices[i]` is the edge between this face and `adjacent_faces[i]`.
- `vertex_indices: Vec<usize>` — BRepVertex indices at corners. `vertex_indices[i]` is the vertex between `edge_indices[i]` and `edge_indices[(i+1) % N]`.
- (TODO) `occt_face: Option<TopoDS_Face>` — Populated in 3.4.

A vector of `ReconEdge` structs:
- `face_indices: [usize; 2]` — Indices of the two adjacent FaceDescriptors.

fn smallest_eigenvector_3x3(m: &[f64; 6]) -> [f64; 3] {
    // m = [[m[0], m[1], m[2]],
    //      [m[1], m[3], m[4]],
    //      [m[2], m[4], m[5]]]
    // Use the characteristic polynomial approach for 3x3 symmetric matrices.
    // Characteristic equation: λ³ - c2*λ² + c1*λ - c0 = 0
    // where c2 = trace, c1 = sum of 2x2 minors, c0 = determinant
    let a00 = m[0]; let a01 = m[1]; let a02 = m[2];
    let a11 = m[3]; let a12 = m[4]; let a22 = m[5];

    let c2 = a00 + a11 + a22;
    let c1 = a00 * a11 - a01 * a01 + a00 * a22 - a02 * a02 + a11 * a22 - a12 * a12;
    let c0 = a00 * (a11 * a22 - a12 * a12)
            - a01 * (a01 * a22 - a12 * a02)
            + a02 * (a01 * a12 - a11 * a02);

    // Depressed cubic: t³ + pt + q = 0 where λ = t + c2/3
    let p = c1 - c2 * c2 / 3.0;
    let q = -2.0 * c2 * c2 * c2 / 27.0 + c1 * c2 / 3.0 - c0;

    let eigenvalues = if p.abs() < 1e-30 {
        // All eigenvalues equal
        let ev = c2 / 3.0;
        [ev, ev, ev]
    } else {
        // p < 0 for real symmetric matrices with distinct eigenvalues
        let neg_p_3 = (-p / 3.0).max(0.0);
        let r = neg_p_3.sqrt();
        let cos_arg = (-q / (2.0 * neg_p_3 * r)).clamp(-1.0, 1.0);
        let theta = cos_arg.acos() / 3.0;
        let shift = c2 / 3.0;
        let two_pi_3 = 2.0 * std::f64::consts::PI / 3.0;
        let ev0 = 2.0 * r * theta.cos() + shift;
        let ev1 = 2.0 * r * (theta - two_pi_3).cos() + shift;
        let ev2 = 2.0 * r * (theta - 2.0 * two_pi_3).cos() + shift;
        [ev0, ev1, ev2]
    };

    // Find the smallest eigenvalue
    let (min_idx, _) = eigenvalues.iter().enumerate()
        .min_by(|a, b| a.1.partial_cmp(b.1).unwrap())
        .unwrap();

    // Compute eigenvector for the smallest eigenvalue using inverse iteration:
    // (M - λI)v = 0, find null space
    // Shift slightly to avoid exact singularity issues
    let lambda = eigenvalues[min_idx];
    let b00 = a00 - lambda;
    let b11 = a11 - lambda;
    let b22 = a22 - lambda;

    // Try cross products of rows to find the null space
    let row0 = [b00, a01, a02];
    let row1 = [a01, b11, a12];
    let row2 = [a02, a12, b22];

    let candidates = [
        cross3(&row0, &row1),
        cross3(&row0, &row2),
        cross3(&row1, &row2),
    ];

    // Pick the cross product with largest magnitude
    let mut best_idx = 0;
    let mut best_len_sq = 0.0;
    for (i, c) in candidates.iter().enumerate() {
        let len_sq = c[0] * c[0] + c[1] * c[1] + c[2] * c[2];
        if len_sq > best_len_sq {
            best_len_sq = len_sq;
            best_idx = i;
        }
    }

    let mut ev = candidates[best_idx];
    let len = normalize3(&mut ev);
    if len < 1e-15 {
        // Fallback: eigenvalues are degenerate, return arbitrary unit vector
        return [1.0, 0.0, 0.0];
    }
    ev
}

/// Fit cylinder parameters (axis direction, axis origin, radius) from a set of faces.
/// Uses face normals for initial axis estimate, then iteratively refines the axis
/// direction by minimizing vertex-to-cylinder distance.
/// Returns (axis_origin, axis_direction, radius).
fn fit_cylinder(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Step 1: Initial axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);
    let mut axis_dir = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);
    let len = normalize3(&mut axis_dir);
    if len < 1e-15 {
        return None;
    }

    // Step 2: Initial circle fit with this axis.
    let (mut axis_origin, mut radius) = fit_circle_for_axis(&axis_dir, vertex_set, vertices)?;

    // Step 3: Iteratively refine axis direction to minimize vertex-to-cylinder SSE.
    // Use Newton-style optimization on two angular parameters (tilt in two
    // perpendicular directions). At each iteration, evaluate SSE at a 5-point
    // stencil (center + ±h along two perp dirs) to estimate gradient and
    // Hessian diagonal, then take a Newton step with trust-region clamping.
    let max_iter = 50;

    let mut best_sse = vertex_set.iter().map(|&vi| {
        let d = vertex_to_cylinder_distance(&vertices[vi], &axis_origin, &axis_dir, radius);
        d * d
    }).sum::<f64>();

    for _ in 0..max_iter {
        let (perp_u, perp_w) = perpendicular_basis(&axis_dir);
        let prev_sse = best_sse;

        // Finite difference step — scale to the problem: use 1e-4 rad (~0.006°)
        let h = 1e-4_f64;

        // Evaluate SSE at 4 perturbed axis directions
        let mut sse_vals = [None; 4]; // +u, -u, +w, -w
        let perps = [perp_u, perp_w];
        for (pi, perp) in perps.iter().enumerate() {
            for (si, sign) in [1.0_f64, -1.0].iter().enumerate() {
                let mut trial_dir = [
                    axis_dir[0] + sign * h * perp[0],
                    axis_dir[1] + sign * h * perp[1],
                    axis_dir[2] + sign * h * perp[2],
                ];
                normalize3(&mut trial_dir);
                sse_vals[pi * 2 + si] = cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices)
                    .map(|(sse, _, _)| sse);
            }
        }

        // For each direction, compute gradient and curvature via central difference
        let mut improved = false;
        for (pi, perp) in perps.iter().enumerate() {
            let sse_plus = match sse_vals[pi * 2] { Some(v) => v, None => continue };
            let sse_minus = match sse_vals[pi * 2 + 1] { Some(v) => v, None => continue };

            let grad = (sse_plus - sse_minus) / (2.0 * h);
            let curv = (sse_plus - 2.0 * best_sse + sse_minus) / (h * h);

            // Newton step: step = -grad/curv, clamped to trust region
            let step = if curv > 1e-30 {
                (-grad / curv).clamp(-0.1, 0.1) // trust region: max 0.1 rad
            } else if grad.abs() > 1e-30 {
                // Curvature too small — use gradient descent with conservative step
                (-grad.signum() * 1e-3).clamp(-0.01, 0.01)
            } else {
                continue;
            };

            let mut trial_dir = [
                axis_dir[0] + step * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] + step * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] + step * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut trial_dir);

            if let Some((trial_sse, trial_origin, trial_radius)) =
                cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices)
            {
                if trial_sse < best_sse {
                    best_sse = trial_sse;
                    axis_dir = trial_dir;
                    axis_origin = trial_origin;
                    radius = trial_radius;

}

/// Helper: check if two faces are pairwise qualified for cylindrical seeding.
/// Both must be unassigned, neighbors, have sufficient normal difference, and
/// dihedral angle within tolerance.
fn is_pairwise_qualified(
    fi: usize,
    ni: usize,
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    angular_tol: f64,
) -> bool {
    if mesh.faces[ni].cylindrical_hypothesis != UNDEDUCED_CYLINDRICAL_HYPOTHESIS {
        eprintln!("  [pairwise] fi={fi} ni={ni}: already assigned hyp={}", mesh.faces[ni].cylindrical_hypothesis);
        return false;
    }
    let fi_normal = match mesh.faces[fi].normal {
        Some(n) => n,
        None => return false,
    };
    let ni_normal = match mesh.faces[ni].normal {
        Some(n) => n,
        None => return false,
    };
    let cross = cross3(&fi_normal, &ni_normal);
    let cross_mag = (cross[0] * cross[0] + cross[1] * cross[1] + cross[2] * cross[2]).sqrt();
    let cos_angle = dot3(&fi_normal, &ni_normal).clamp(-1.0, 1.0);
    if cross_mag < MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD {
        return false;
    }
    let angle = cos_angle.acos();
    eprintln!("  [pairwise] fi={fi} ni={ni}: cross_mag={cross_mag:.4} angle={:.1}°", angle.to_degrees());
    angle <= angular_tol
}

/// Deduce cylindrical hypotheses from the mesh using 3-face seed BFS evaluation.
///
/// For each face fi, finds triples (fi, n1, n2) where n1 is a pairwise-qualified
/// neighbor of fi, and n2 is a pairwise-qualified neighbor of n1 that is NOT a
/// neighbor of fi. Runs a trial BFS for each triple, keeps the best trial
/// (most area), and commits.
fn deduce_cylindrical_hypotheses(
    mesh: &mut ConnectedMesh,
    vertex_tol: f64,
    surface_tol: f64,
    angular_tol: f64,

            .filter(|&&n| n >= 0)
            .map(|&n| n as usize)
            .collect()

Returns eigenvalues in unspecified order.\nfn eigenvalues_3x3_symmetric(m: &[f64; 6]) -> [f64; 3] {\n    let a00 = m[0]; let a01 = m[1]; let a02 = m[2];\n    let a11 = m[3]; let a12 = m[4]; let a22 = m[5];\n\n    let c2 = a00 + a11 + a22;\n    let c1 = a00 * a11 - a01 * a01 + a00 * a22 - a02 * a02 + a11 * a22 - a12 * a12;\n    let c0 = a00 * (a11 * a22 - a12 * a12)\n            - a01 * (a01 * a22 - a12 * a02)\n            + a02 * (a01 * a12 - a11 * a02);\n\n    let p = c1 - c2 * c2 / 3.0;\n    let q = -2.0 * c2 * c2 * c2 / 27.0 + c1 * c2 / 3.0 - c0;\n\n    if p.abs() < 1e-30 {\n        let ev = c2 / 3.0;\n        return [ev, ev, ev];\n    }\n\n    let neg_p_3 = (-p / 3.0).max(0.0);\n    let r = neg_p_3.sqrt();\n    let cos_arg = (-q / (2.0 * neg_p_3 * r)).clamp(-1.0, 1.0);\n    let theta = cos_arg.acos() / 3.0;\n    let shift = c2 / 3.0;\n    let two_pi_3 = 2.0 * std::f64::consts::PI / 3.0;\n    [2.0 * r * theta.cos() + shift,\n     2.0 * r * (theta - two_pi_3).cos() + shift,\n     2.0 * r * (theta - 2.0 * two_pi_3).cos() + shift]\n}\n\n/// Compute the eigenvector of a 3x3 symmetric matrix for a given eigenvalue.\n/// Uses cross products of (M - λI) rows to find the null space.\nfn eigenvector_for_eigenvalue(m: &[f64; /// Fit cylinder parameters (axis direction, axis origin, radius) from a set of faces.
/// Uses face normals for initial axis estimate, then iteratively refines the axis
/// direction by minimizing vertex-to-cylinder distance.
/// Returns (axis_origin, axis_direction, radius).
fn fit_cylinder(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Step 1: Initial axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);
    let mut axis_dir = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);
    let len = normalize3(&mut axis_dir);
    if len < 1e-15 {
        return None;
    }

    // Step 2: Initial circle fit with this axis.
    let (mut axis_origin, mut radius) = fit_circle_for_axis(&axis_dir, vertex_set, vertices)?;

    // Step 3: Iteratively refine axis direction to minimize vertex-to-cylinder SSE.
    // Use Newton-style optimization on two angular parameters (tilt in two
    // perpendicular directions). At each iteration, evaluate SSE at a 5-point
    // stencil (center + ±h along two perp dirs) to estimate gradient and
    // Hessian diagonal, then take a Newton step with trust-region clamping.
    let max_iter = 50;

    let mut best_sse = vertex_set.iter().map(|&vi| {
        let d = vertex_to_cylinder_distance(&vertices[vi], &axis_origin, &axis_dir, radius);
        d * d
    }).sum::<f64>();

    for _ in 0..max_iter {
        let (perp_u, perp_w) = perpendicular_basis(&axis_dir);
        let prev_sse = best_sse;

        // Finite difference step — scale to the problem: use 1e-4 rad (~0.006°)
        let h = 1e-4_f64;

        // Evaluate SSE at 4 perturbed axis directions
        let mut sse_vals = [None; 4]; // +u, -u, +w, -w
        let perps = [perp_u, perp_w];
        for (pi, perp) in perps.iter().enumerate() {
            for (si, sign) in [1.0_f64, -1.0].iter().enumerate() {
                let mut trial_dir = [
                    axis_dir[0] + sign * h * perp[0],
                    axis_dir[1] + sign * h * perp[1],
                    axis_dir[2] + sign * h * perp[2],
                ];
                normalize3(&mut trial_dir);
                sse_vals[pi * 2 + si] = cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices)
                    .map(|(sse, _, _)| sse);
            }
        }

        // For each direction, compute gradient and curvature via central difference
        let mut improved = false;
        for (pi, perp) in perps.iter().enumerate() {
            let sse_plus = match sse_vals[pi * 2] { Some(v) => v, None => continue };
            let sse_minus = match sse_vals[pi * 2 + 1] { Some(v) => v, None => continue };

            let grad = (sse_plus - sse_minus) / (2.0 * h);
            let curv = (sse_plus - 2.0 * best_sse + sse_minus) / (h * h);

            // Newton step: step = -grad/curv, clamped to trust region
            let step = if curv > 1e-30 {
                (-grad / curv).clamp(-0.1, 0.1) // trust region: max 0.1 rad
            } else if grad.abs() > 1e-30 {
                // Curvature too small — use gradient descent with conservative step
                (-grad.signum() * 1e-3).clamp(-0.01, 0.01)
            } else {
                continue;
            };

            let mut trial_dir = [
                axis_dir[0] + step * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] + step * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] + step * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut trial_dir);

            if let Some((trial_sse, trial_origin, trial_radius)) =
                cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices)
            {
                if trial_sse < best_sse {
                    best_sse = trial_sse;
                    axis_dir = trial_dir;
                    axis_origin = trial_origi

    {
      "content": "/// Fit cylinder parameters (axis direction, axis origin, radius) from a set of faces.\n/// Tries two axis estimation strategies — normal-covariance (good for wide arcs) and\n/// vertex-PCA (good for narrow arcs / drum-laced triangles) — and picks the one\n/// with lower sum of squared vertex-to-cylinder distances.\n/// Returns (axis_origin, axis_direction, radius).\nfn fit_cylinder(\n    face_indices: &[usize],\n    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,\n    faces: &[MeshFace],\n    vertices: &[MeshVertex],\n) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {\n    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {\n        return None;\n    }\n\n    // Candidate A: Axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.\n    // Works well when face normals span a wide arc of the cylinder.\n    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);\n    let mut axis_a = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);\n    let len_a = normalize3(&mut axis_a);\n    let fit_a = if len_a > 1e-15 {\n        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_a, vertex_set, vertices)\n    } else {\n        None\n    };\n\n    // Candidate B: Axis from largest eigenvector of vertex position covariance.\n    // Works well for narrow-arc drum-laced triangles where the axial extent\n    // exceeds the circumferential arc, making the axis the direction of\n    // maximum vertex spread.\n    let vert_cov = build_vertex_covariance(vertex_set, vertices);\n    let mut axis_b = largest_eigenvector_3x3(&vert_cov);\n    let len_b = normalize3(&mut axis_b);\n    let fit_b = if len_b > 1e-15 {\n        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_b, vertex_set, vertices)\n    } else {\n        None\n    };\n\n    // Pick the axis candidate with lower SSE.\n    match (fit_a, fit_b) {\n        (Some((sse_a, origin_a, r_a)), Some((sse_b, origin_b, r_b))) => {\n            if sse_a <= sse_b {\n                Some((origin_a, axis_a, r_a))\n            } else {\n                Some((origin_b, axis_b, r_b))\n            }\n        }\n        (Some((_, origin, r)), None) => Some((origin, axis_a, r)),\n        (None, Some((_, origin, r))) => Some((origin, axis_b, r)),\n        (None, None) => None,\n    }\n}",

```
/// Fit cylinder parameters (axis direction, axis origin, radius) from a set of faces.
/// Tries two axis estimation strategies — normal-covariance (good for wide arcs) and
/// vertex-PCA (good for narrow arcs / drum-laced triangles) — and picks the one
/// with lower sum of squared vertex-to-cylinder distances.
/// Returns (axis_origin, axis_direction, radius).
fn fit_cylinder(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Candidate A: Axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    // Works well when face normals span a wide arc of the cylinder.
    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);
    let mut axis_a = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);
    let len_a = normalize3(&mut axis_a);
    let fit_a = if len_a > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_a, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Candidate B: Axis from largest eigenvector of vertex position covariance.
    // Works well for narrow-arc drum-laced triangles where the axial extent
    // exceeds the circumferential arc, making the axis the direction of
    // maximum vertex spread.
    let vert_cov = build_vertex_covariance(vertex_set, vertices);
    let mut axis_b = largest_eigenvector_3x3(&vert_cov);
    let len_b = normalize3(&mut axis_b);
    let fit_b = if len_b > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_b, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Pick the axis candidate with lower SSE.
    match (fit_a, fit_b) {
        (Some((sse_a, origin_a, r_a)), Some((sse_b, origin_b, r_b))) => {
            if sse_a <= sse_b {
                Some((origin_a, axis_a, r_a))
            } else {
                Some((origin_b, axis_b, r_b))
            }
        }
        (Some((_, origin, r)), None) => Some((origin, axis_a, r)),
        (None, Some((_, origin, r))) => Some((origin, axis_b, r)),
        (None, None) => None,
    }
}

/// Check if all vertices in a set are within tolerance of a cylinder surface.
fn all_vertices_within_cylinder_tolerance(
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    axis_origin: &[f64; 3],
    axis_direction: &[f64; 3],
```

```
fn vertex_to_cylinder_distance(
    v: &MeshVertex,
    axis_origin: &[f64; 3],
    axis_direction: &[f64; 3],
    radius: f64,
) -> f64 {
    // Vector from axis origin to vertex
    let dx = v.x - axis_origin[0];
    let dy = v.y - axis_origin[1];
    let dz = v.z - axis_origin[2];
    // Project onto axis
    let t = dx * axis_direction[0] + dy * axis_direction[1] + dz * axis_direction[2];
    // Radial vector (perpendicular to axis)
    let rx = dx - t * axis_direction[0];
    let ry = dy - t * axis_direction[1];
    let rz = dz - t * axis_direction[2];
    let radial_dist = (rx * rx + ry * ry + rz * rz).sqrt();
    radial_dist - radius
}

/// Normalize a 3D vector in place, return its length.
fn normalize3(v: &mut [f64; 3]) -> f64 {
    let len = (v[0] * v[0] + v[1] * v[1] + v[2] * v[2]).sqrt();
    if len > 0.0 {
        v[0] /= len;
        v[1] /= len;
        v[2] /= len;
    }
    len
}

/// Cross product of two 3D vectors.
fn cross3(a: &[f64; 3], b: &[f64; 3]) -> [f64; 3] {
    [
        a[1] * b[2] - a[2] * b[1],
        a[2] * b[0] - a[0] * b[2],
        a[0] * b[1] - a[1] * b[0],
    ]
```

> The normal covariance axis is actually quite accurate directionally, but the fitted radius of 1.867 is noticeably short of the expected 2.0, whereas the Python analysis with the true axis [0, 0, 1] yielded r ≈ 1.999994. This suggests that even a small 0.41° axis deviation is enough to pull the radius estimate down significantly.
> 
> The issue becomes clear when I consider the geometry: with only ~15° of arc coverage and a 6.5mm z-span, a 0.41° tilt causes the projected vertex positions to shift by roughly 0.047mm—which is about 20% of the chord length. This distortion is substantial enough to noticeably alter the circle fit, pushing the radius estimate lower. For such narrow arc spans, axis precision becomes critical because even minor tilts create outsized effects on the fitted radius.
> 
> The core problem is that the normal-covariance method gives a reasonably accurate axis (0.41° error), but the radius calculation remains extremely sensitive to this small error. Meanwhile, the vertex PCA approach fails because the points are elongated along the z-axis, leaving almost no xy-spread to work with—making the fit vulnerable to noise and centroid offsets. The real solution requires recognizing that when the axis is already very close to [0,0,1], I need a different refinement strategy that doesn't amplify small axis errors into large radius errors. The tilt of 0.41° shifts the projected positions by roughly 0.047mm between top and bottom vertices, but the arc chord is only about 0.24mm, so this creates a ~20% systematic distortion that throws off the circle fit. I could push for much higher axis accuracy below 0.01°, use a less sensitive fitting method, or let the BFS expansion correct things as more faces get added and constrain the fit better. The third approach seems most practical since the code already refits when new faces join, but the problem is the seed-stage vertex tolerance check is rejecting candidates before BFS even gets a chance to improve things.
> 
> The seed produces a radius of 1.867 with a worst distance of 9.47e-3, which blows past the 1e-5 tolerance threshold. I need to relax the vertex tolerance at the seed stage to let BFS proceed, then apply stricter checks once enough faces have been added. Looking at the code, the seed verification step checks whether all vertices stay within the cylinder tolerance before allowing the trial to continue.
> 
> Checking the tolerance enforcement...
> 
> Actually, relaxing the seed tolerance might flood the search with bad candidates. A smarter approach would be to base the seed tolerance on the mesh's angular resolution — for a cylinder with radius r and angular tolerance θ, the characteristic facet height is r * (1 - cos(θ/2)). With r=2mm and θ=17.5°, that gives roughly 0.023mm, which is much larger than the current 9.47e-3 worst distance.
> 
> But the real issue is that vertex tolerance shouldn't be 1e-5 — vertices on a properly tessellated mesh are essentially on the CAD surface. The problem arises when the axis estimate is wrong, causing fitted vertices to deviate. For a cylinder with axis error ε and z-span L, the vertex-to-cylinder distance scales roughly as L * sin(ε), so with L=6.5mm and ε=0.005 rad, I'm looking at deviations around 0.033mm.
> 
> Rather than guessing at a seed tolerance, I should use a practical approach: take the maximum of the vertex tolerance and some fraction of the fitted radius, like 1% of the radius. For the current case with radius 1.867mm and worst distance of 0.00947mm, that's a ratio of 0.5%, which fits comfortably within a 1% tolerance threshold.
> 
> Looking at the other seed candidates for face 116, most have either wildly incorrect radii (20+ mm instead of ~2mm) or much worse fits, so a tolerance scaled to the radius would naturally reject those while accepting the good 3-face seed. I'm considering whether to use `surface_tolerance` instead of `vertex_tolerance` for the seed check, but that would essentially bypass the seed validation entirely since 0.4mm is so permissive. Looking at the actual validation flow, the seed vertex check is just the first gate—wrong cylinders get filtered out later by centroid validation and angular coverage checks during the BFS expansion phase.
> 
> The key insight is that during BFS, when each new face is added, the cylinder gets refit with an improved axis, and then all vertices are checked against `vertex_tolerance` again. So the seed-stage check is somewhat redundant with the BFS-stage check, except the BFS check uses a better-fitted axis. My approach should be to relax the seed-stage tolerance and let BFS do the real filtering work with its more accurate refits.

The issue is that with 5 vertices spanning only ~15° of arc, even a 0.41° axis error causes a 7% radius error because the projected vertex arc is so narrow. The vertex tolerance check (1e-5) rejects this before BFS can expand and improve the fit.

The fix: use a relaxed tolerance at the seed stage. Let me check the BFS expansion code to understand how it re-validates:

    // Compute angular coordinate for each face centroid
    let mut thetas: Vec<f64> = Vec::with_capacity(face_list.len());
    for &fi in face_list {
        let c = face_centroid(&faces[fi], vertices);
        let d = [
            c[0] - axis_origin[0],
            c[1] - axis_origin[1],
            c[2] - axis_origin[2],
        ];
        let t = dot3(&d, axis_direction);
        let radial = [
            d[0] - t * axis_direction[0],
            d[1] - t * axis_direction[1],
            d[2] - t * axis_direction[2],
        ];
        let theta = f64::atan2(dot3(&radial, &w), dot3(&radial, &u));
        thetas.push(theta);
    }

    thetas.sort_by(|a, b| a.partial_cmp(b).unwrap());
    let n = thetas.len();

    // Compute all gaps (including wraparound)
    let mut gaps: Vec<f64> = Vec::with_capacity(n);
    for i in 1..n {
        gaps.push(thetas[i] - thetas[i - 1]);
    }
    // Wraparound gap
    gaps.push(thetas[0] + 2.0 * std::f64::consts::PI - thetas[n - 1]);

    // Find largest and second-largest gaps
    let mut largest = 0.0_f64;
    let mut second_largest = 0.0_f64;
    for &g in &gaps {
        if g > largest {
            second_largest = largest;
            largest = g;
        } else if g > second_largest {
            second_largest = g;
        }
    }

    let span = 2.0 * std::f64::consts::PI - largest;
    if span <= 0.0 {
        return false;
    }

    second_largest <= span / 3.0
}

/// Result of a trial BFS for cylindrical hypothesis evaluation.
struct CylinderTrialResult {
    faces: Vec<usize>,
    vertices: HashSet<usize>,
    axis_origin: [f64; 3],
    axis_direction: [f64; 3],
    radius: f64,
    convex: bool,
    error_max: f64,
    centroid_error_max: f64,
    error_abs_sum: f64,
    total_area: f64,
}

/// Run a trial BFS for a cylindrical hypothesis starting from seed faces,
/// using temporary data structures (not mutating mesh face assignments).
/// Returns `None` if the trial fails validation (min faces, centroid check).
/// Sets `viz_quit` if the user quits from viz.
#[allow(clippy::too_many_arguments)]
fn run_cylinder_trial_bfs(
    seed_faces: &[usize],
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    vertex_tol: f64,
    surface_tol: f64,
    angular_tol: f64,
    verbosity: u8,
    viz: Option<&VizSender>,
    viz_quit: &std::cell::Cell<bool>,
) -> Option<CylinderTrialResult> {
    // Collect seed vertices from all seed faces
    let mut vertex_set: HashSet<usize> = HashSet::new();
    for &sfi in seed_faces {
        let vc = mesh.faces[sfi].vertex_count as usize;
        for &vi in &mesh.faces[sfi].vertex_indices[..vc] {
            vertex_set.insert(vi);
        }
    }

    let mut face_list: Vec<usize> = seed_faces.to_vec();

    // Fit cylinder to seed faces
    let (mut current_origin, mut current_dir, mut current_radius) = match
        fit_cylinder(&face_list, &vertex_set, &mesh.faces, &mesh.vertices)
    {
        Some(v) => v,
        None => {
            if verbosity >= 3 {
                let seed_str: Vec<String> = seed_faces.iter().map(|f| f.to_string()).collect();
                eprintln!("  [BFS-cyl] seed=({}) fit_cylinder returned None (degenerate geometry)", seed_str.join(","));
            }
            return None;

> 💭 Now I understand the full flow. The key observation is:
> 
> 1. **Seed vertex tolerance check** (line 1380-1398): Uses strict `vertex_tol` (1e-5mm). If the seed fit gives worst_dist=9.47e-3, it fails and the trial is immediately rejected.
> 
> 2. **BFS expansion** (line 1588-1674): New faces are checked against `vertex_tol`, but if they're within `REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER * vertex_tol`, a refit is triggered. The refit uses ALL faces (seed + new), giving a better axis.
> 
> 
> 3. **Final refit** (line 1777): After BFS completes, refits with all faces.
> 
> The root issue is that the seed-stage check is too restrictive—it rejects candidates before BFS can even start and improve the fit. I need to relax this constraint, either by switching to `surface_tol`, applying a multiplier to `vertex_tol`, or skipping the check entirely and relying on centroid and angular coverage validation instead.
> 
> The centroid check already uses `surface_tol` (0.4mm default) and would still filter bad seeds, so removing the vertex tolerance check is viable. But I want to avoid wasting time on obviously bad seeds. A better approach: use the maximum of `vertex_tol` and 0.01 times the radius—for a radius of 2mm, that gives 0.02mm, which is reasonable for seed-stage validation and would accept the current worst_dist of 9.47e-3. —reject this. After the BFS expansion, the final worst_dist needs to stay within vertex_tol (1e-5) for acceptance. If the initial fit is poor (like r=1.867 instead of 2.0), the BFS might pull in wrong faces since it's checking against the current cylinder's vertex_tol, which could reject faces that actually belong to the correct cylinder. There's a refit mechanism that kicks in when faces are close but not quite within tolerance, though it gets cut off here.
> 
> Now I'm checking what the refit multiplier threshold is and whether it's large enough to catch faces that are far from the wrong cylinder.

> 💭 So `REFIT_SKIP_MULTIPLIER = 2.0`. During BFS, a face is:
> - Directly accepted if ALL vertices within `vertex_tol` (1e-5)

    // Final re-fit
    if let Some((final_origin, final_dir, final_radius)) =
        fit_cylinder(&face_list, &vertex_set, &mesh.faces, &mesh.vertices)
    {
        current_origin = final_origin;
        current_dir = final_dir;
        current_radius = final_radius;
    }

    // Compute error metrics
    let mut error_max = 0.0_f64;
    let mut error_abs_sum = 0.0_f64;
    for &vi in &vertex_set {
        let d = vertex_to_cylinder_distance(
            &mesh.vertices[vi], &current_origin, &current_dir, current_radius,
        ).abs();
        error_max = error_max.max(d);
        error_abs_sum += d;
    }

    // Validate: minimum face count
    if face_list.len() < MIN_CYLINDER_FACES {
        return None;
    }

    // Validate: centroid check; also compute centroid_error_max
    let mut centroid_error_max = 0.0_f64;
    for &f in &face_list {
        let c = face_centroid(&mesh.faces[f], &mesh.vertices);
        let d = vertex_to_cylinder_distance(
            &MeshVertex::from_xyz(c[0], c[1], c[2]),
            &current_origin, &current_dir, current_radius,
        ).abs();
        centroid_error_max = centroid_error_max.max(d);
        if d > surface_tol {
            return None;
        }
    }

    // Compute total area for area-based comparison
    let total_area: f64 = face_list.iter().map(|&f| face_area(&mesh.faces[f], &mesh.vertices)).sum();

    Some(CylinderTrialResult {
        faces: face_list,
        vertices: vertex_set,
        axis_origin: current_origin,
        axis_direction: current_dir,
        radius: current_radius,
        convex,
        error_max,
        centroid_error_max,
        error_abs_sum,
        total_area,
    })
}

/// Fit cylinder parameters (axis direction, axis origin, radius) from a set of faces.
/// Tries two axis estimation strategies — normal-covariance (good for wide arcs) and
/// vertex-PCA (good for narrow arcs / drum-laced triangles) — and picks the one
/// with lower sum of squared vertex-to-cylinder distances.
/// Returns (axis_origin, axis_direction, radius).
fn fit_cylinder(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Candidate A: Axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    // Works well when face normals span a wide arc of the cylinder.
    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);
    let mut axis_a = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);
    let len_a = normalize3(&mut axis_a);
    let fit_a = if len_a > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_a, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Candidate B: Axis from largest eigenvector of vertex position covariance.
    // Works well for narrow-arc drum-laced triangles where the axial extent
    // exceeds the circumferential arc, making the axis the direction of
    // maximum vertex spread.
    let vert_cov = build_vertex_covariance(vertex_set, vertices);
    let mut axis_b = largest_eigenvector_3x3(&vert_cov);
    let len_b = normalize3(&mut axis_b);
    let fit_b = if len_b > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_b, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Pick the axis candidate with lower SSE.
    eprintln!("  [fit_cylinder] faces={:?} axis_a=[{:.6},{:.6},{:.6}] sse_a={:?} r_a={:?}",
        face_indices, axis_a[0], axis_a[1], axis_a[2], fit_a.map(|(s,_,_)| s), fit_a.map(|(_,_,r)| r));
    eprintln!("  [fit_cylinder] faces={:?} axis_b=[{:.6},{:.6},{:.6}] sse_b={:?} r_b={:?}",
        face_indices, axis_b[0], axis_b[1], axis_b[2], fit_b.map(|(s,_,_)| s), fit_b.map(|(_,_,r)| r));
    match (fit_a, fit_b) {
        (Some((sse_a, origin_a, r_a)), Some((sse_b, origin_b, r_b))) => {
            if sse_a <= sse_b {
                Some((origin_a, axis_a, r_a))
            } else {
                Some((origin_b, axis_b, r_b))
            }
        }
        (Some((_, origin, r)), None) => Some((origin, axis_a, r)),
        (None, Some((_, origin, r))) => Some((origin, axis_b, r)),
        (None, None) => None,
    }
}

/// Check if all vertices in a set are within tolerance of a cylinder surface.
```

    // Refine axis direction via Newton-style optimization.
    // Use h=0.01 rad (~0.57°) for finite differences — small enough for
    // accuracy but large enough to avoid cancellation error in the curvature
    // estimate. For narrow-arc seed triangles, the SSE landscape is smooth
    // and nearly quadratic, so a few Newton iterations converge quickly.
    let h = 0.01_f64;
    for _ in 0..10 {
        let (perp_u, perp_w) = perpendicular_basis(&axis_dir);
        let prev_sse = best_sse;

        for perp in &[perp_u, perp_w] {
            let mut dir_plus = [
                axis_dir[0] + h * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] + h * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] + h * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut dir_plus);
            let mut dir_minus = [
                axis_dir[0] - h * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] - h * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] - h * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut dir_minus);

            let sse_plus = match cylinder_fit_sse(&dir_plus, vertex_set, vertices) {
                Some((s, _, _)) => s,
                None => continue,
            };
            let sse_minus = match cylinder_fit_sse(&dir_minus, vertex_set, vertices) {
                Some((s, _, _)) => s,
                None => continue,
            };

            let grad = (sse_plus - sse_minus) / (2.0 * h);
            let curv = (sse_plus - 2.0 * best_sse + sse_minus) / (h * h);

            let step = if curv > 1e-20 {
                (-grad / curv).clamp(-0.1, 0.1)
            } else {
                continue;
            };
            if step.abs() < 1e-12 {
                continue;
            }

            let mut trial_dir = [
                axis_dir[0] + step * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] + step * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] + step * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut trial_dir);

            if let Some((trial_sse, trial_origin, trial_radius)) =
                cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices)
            {
                if trial_sse < best_sse {
                    best_sse = trial_sse;
                    axis_dir = trial_dir;
                    axis_origin = trial_origin;
                    radius = trial_radius;
                }
            }
        }

        if (prev_sse - best_sse).abs() < 1e-30 {
            break;
        }
    }

fn perpendicular_basis(d: &[f64; 3]) -> ([f64; 3], [f64; 3]) {
    let (ax, ay, az) = (d[0].abs(), d[1].abs(), d[2].abs());
    let perp = if ax <= ay && ax <= az {
        [1.0, 0.0, 0.0]
    } else if ay <= az {
        [0.0, 1.0, 0.0]
    } else {
        [0.0, 0.0, 1.0]
    };
    let mut u = cross3(d, &perp);
    normalize3(&mut u);
    let w = cross3(d, &u);
    (u, w)
}

/// Validate that faces in a cylindrical hypothesis have sufficient angular
/// coverage around the cylinder — i.e., they are distributed around the
/// circumference, not clustered on one side.
///
/// Requires at least 3 distinct angular clusters. The check is:
/// - Compute the angular coordinate θ of each face centroid around the axis.
/// - Sort θ values and compute all N inter-face gaps plus the wraparound gap.
/// - The largest gap defines the "empty arc"; span = 2π − largest_gap.
/// - The second-largest gap must be ≤ span / 3.
fn angular_coverage_valid(
    face_list: &[usize],
    axis_origin: &[f64; 3],
    axis_direction: &[f64; 3],
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> bool {
    if face_list.len() < 3 {
        return false;
    }

    let (u, w) = perpendicular_basis(axis_direction);

I see the issue now. The SSE landscape is non-convex — the Newton refinement follows a local valley to a local minimum at SSE=2.59e-5, while the global minimum is at SSE=3.17e-13 (true axis [0,0,1]). The refinement goes in the WRONG direction (increasing y-tilt to -0.012 instead of reducing it toward 0).

The fix: replace Newton refinement with a **multi-scale grid search** over axis tilt angles. This reliably finds the global minimum regardless of the SSE landscape shape:

    // Debug: check SSE at axis [0,0,1] for known test case
    if face_indices == &[116, 118, 119] {
        let test_dir = [0.0, 0.0, 1.0];
        if let Some((test_sse, test_origin, test_r)) = cylinder_fit_sse(&test_dir, vertex_set, vertices) {
            eprintln!("  [fit_cylinder DEBUG] axis=[0,0,1] sse={:.6e} r={:.6} origin=[{:.6},{:.6},{:.6}]",
                test_sse, test_r, test_origin[0], test_origin[1], test_origin[2]);
        }
        eprintln!("  [fit_cylinder DEBUG] best axis=[{:.6},{:.6},{:.6}] sse={:.6e} r={:.6}",
            axis_dir[0], axis_dir[1], axis_dir[2], best_sse, radius);
    }

    // Refine axis direction via Newton-style optimization.
    // Use h=0.01 rad (~0.57°) for finite differences — small enough for
    // accuracy but large enough to avoid cancellation error in the curvature
    // estimate. For narrow-arc seed triangles, the SSE landscape is smooth
    // and nearly quadratic, so a few Newton iterations converge quickly.
    let h = 0.01_f64;
    for _ in 0..10 {
        let (perp_u, perp_w) = perpendicular_basis(&axis_dir);
        let prev_sse = best_sse;

        for perp in &[perp_u, perp_w] {
            let mut dir_plus = [
                axis_dir[0] + h * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] + h * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] + h * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut dir_plus);
            let mut dir_minus = [
                axis_dir[0] - h * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] - h * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] - h * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut dir_minus);

            let sse_plus = match cylinder_fit_sse(&dir_plus, vertex_set, vertices) {
                Some((s, _, _)) => s,
                None => continue,
            };
            let sse_minus = match cylinder_fit_sse(&dir_minus, vertex_set, vertices) {
                Some((s, _, _)) => s,
                None => continue,
            };

            let grad = (sse_plus - sse_minus) / (2.0 * h);
            let curv = (sse_plus - 2.0 * best_sse + sse_minus) / (h * h);

            eprintln!("    [refine] best_sse={:.6e} sse+={:.6e} sse-={:.6e} grad={:.6e} curv={:.6e}", best_sse, sse_plus, sse_minus, grad, curv);

            let step = if curv > 1e-20 {
                (-grad / curv).clamp(-0.1, 0.1)
            } else {
                continue;
            };
            eprintln!("    [refine] step={:.6e}", step);
            if step.abs() < 1e-12 {
                continue;
            }

            let mut trial_dir = [
                axis_dir[0] + step * perp[0],
                axis_dir[1] + step * perp[1],
                axis_dir[2] + step * perp[2],
            ];
            normalize3(&mut trial_dir);

            if let Some((trial_sse, trial_origin, trial_radius)) =
                cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices)
            {
                if trial_sse < best_sse {
                    best_sse = trial_sse;
                    axis_dir = trial_dir;
                    axis_origin = trial_origin;
                    radius = trial_radius;
                }
            }
        }

        if (prev_sse - best_sse).abs() < 1e-30 {
            break;
        }

/// Dot product of two 3D vectors.
fn dot3(a: &[f64; 3], b: &[f64; 3]) -> f64 {
    a[0] * b[0] + a[1] * b[1] + a[2] * b[2]
}

/// Compute the smallest eigenvector of a 3x3 symmetric matrix.
/// The matrix is stored as [m00, m01, m02, m11, m12, m22] (upper triangle).
/// Returns the eigenvector corresponding to the smallest eigenvalue.
/// Compute eigenvalues of a 3x3 symmetric matrix stored as upper triangle
/// [m00, m01, m02, m11, m12, m22]. Returns eigenvalues in unspecified order.
fn eigenvalues_3x3_symmetric(m: &[f64; 6]) -> [f64; 3] {
    let a00 = m[0]; let a01 = m[1]; let a02 = m[2];
    let a11 = m[3]; let a12 = m[4]; let a22 = m[5];

    let c2 = a00 + a11 + a22;
    let c1 = a00 * a11 - a01 * a01 + a00 * a22 - a02 * a02 + a11 * a22 - a12 * a12;
    let c0 = a00 * (a11 * a22 - a12 * a12)
            - a01 * (a01 * a22 - a12 * a02)
            + a02 * (a01 * a12 - a11 * a02);

    let p = c1 - c2 * c2 / 3.0;
    let q = -2.0 * c2 * c2 * c2 / 27.0 + c1 * c2 / 3.0 - c0;

    if p.abs() < 1e-30 {
        let ev = c2 / 3.0;
        return [ev, ev, ev];
    }

    let neg_p_3 = (-p / 3.0).max(0.0);
    let r = neg_p_3.sqrt();
    let cos_arg = (-q / (2.0 * neg_p_3 * r)).clamp(-1.0, 1.0);
    let theta = cos_arg.acos() / 3.0;
    let shift = c2 / 3.0;
    let two_pi_3 = 2.0 * std::f64::consts::PI / 3.0;
    [2.0 * r * theta.cos() + shift,
     2.0 * r * (theta - two_pi_3).cos() + shift,
     2.0 * r * (theta - 2.0 * two_pi_3).cos() + shift]
}

/// Compute the eigenvector of a 3x3 symmetric matrix for a given eigenvalue.
/// Uses cross products of (M - λI) rows to find the null space.
fn eigenvector_for_eigenvalue(m: &[f64; 6], lambda: f64) -> [f64; 3] {
    let b00 = m[0] - lambda;
    let b11 = m[3] - lambda;
    let b22 = m[5] - lambda;

    let row0 = [b00, m[1], m[2]];
    let row1 = [m[1], b11, m[4]];
    let row2 = [m[2], m[4], b22];

    let candidates = [
        cross3(&row0, &row1),
        cross3(&row0, &row2),
        cross3(&row1, &row2),
    ];

    let mut best_idx = 0;
    let mut best_len_sq = 0.0;
    for (i, c) in candidates.iter().enumerate() {
        let len_sq = c[0] * c[0] + c[1] * c[1] + c[2] * c[2];
        if len_sq > best_len_sq {
            best_len_sq = len_sq;
            best_idx = i;
        }
    }

    let mut ev = candidates[best_idx];
    let len = normalize3(&mut ev);
    if len < 1e-15 {
        return [1.0, 0.0, 0.0];
    }
    ev
}

/// Returns the eigenvector corresponding to the smallest eigenvalue.
fn smallest_eigenvector_3x3(m: &[f64; 6]) -> [f64; 3] {
    let eigenvalues = eigenvalues_3x3_symmetric(m);
    let (min_idx, _) = eigenvalues.iter().enumerate()
        .min_by(|a, b| a.1.partial_cmp(b.1).unwrap())
        .unwrap();
    eigenvector_for_eigenvalue(m, eigenvalues[min_idx])
}

/// Returns the eigenvector corresponding to the largest eigenvalue.
fn largest_eigenvector_3x3(m: &[f64; 6]) -> [f64; 3] {
    let eigenvalues = eigenvalues_3x3_symmetric(m);
    let (max_idx, _) = eigenvalues.iter().enumerate()
        .max_by(|a, b| a.1.partial_cmp(b.1).unwrap())
        .unwrap();
    eigenvector_for_eigenvalue(m, eigenvalues[max_idx])
}

/// Build the area-weighted normal covariance matrix M = Σ wᵢ nᵢ nᵢᵀ.
/// Returns the upper triangle [m00, m01, m02, m11, m12, m22].
fn build_normal_covariance(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> [f64; 6] {
    let mut m = [0.0_f64; 6];
    for &fi in face_indices {
        let face = &faces[fi];
        let n = face.normal.unwrap();
        let w = face_area(face, vertices);
        m[0] += w * n[0] * n[0];
        m[1] += w * n[0] * n[1];
        m[2] += w * n[0] * n[2];
        m[3] += w * n[1] * n[1];
        m[4] += w * n[1] * n[2];
        m[5] += w * n[2] * n[2];
    }
    m
}

```

/// Compute the distance from a vertex to a cylinder surface.
/// Returns the signed distance: positive if outside the cylinder, negative if inside.
fn vertex_to_cylinder_distance(
    v: &MeshVertex,
    axis_origin: &[f64; 3],
    axis_direction: &[f64; 3],
    radius: f64,
) -> f64 {
    // Vector from axis origin to vertex
    let dx = v.x - axis_origin[0];
    let dy = v.y - axis_origin[1];
    let dz = v.z - axis_origin[2];
    // Project onto axis
    let t = dx * axis_direction[0] + dy * axis_direction[1] + dz * axis_direction[2];
    // Radial vector (perpendicular to axis)
    let rx = dx - t * axis_direction[0];
    let ry = dy - t * axis_direction[1];
    let rz = dz - t * axis_direction[2];
    let radial_dist = (rx * rx + ry * ry + rz * rz).sqrt();
    radial_dist - radius
}

/// Normalize a 3D vector in place, return its length.
fn normalize3(v: &mut [f64; 3]) -> f64 {
    let len = (v[0] * v[0] + v[1] * v[1] + v[2] * v[2]).sqrt();
    if len > 0.0 {
        v[0] /= len;
        v[1] /= len;
        v[2] /= len;
    }
    len
}

/// Cross product of two 3D vectors.
fn cross3(a: &[f64; 3], b: &[f64; 3]) -> [f64; 3] {
    [
        a[1] * b[2] - a[2] * b[1],
        a[2] * b[0] - a[0] * b[2],
        a[0] * b[1] - a[1] * b[0],
    ]
}

```

fn run_cylinder_trial_bfs(
    seed_faces: &[usize],
    mesh: &ConnectedMesh,
    vertex_tol: f64,
    surface_tol: f64,
    angular_tol: f64,
    verbosity: u8,
    viz: Option<&VizSender>,
    viz_quit: &std::cell::Cell<bool>,
) -> Option<CylinderTrialResult> {
    // Collect seed vertices from all seed faces
    let mut vertex_set: HashSet<usize> = HashSet::new();
    for &sfi in seed_faces {
        let vc = mesh.faces[sfi].vertex_count as usize;
        for &vi in &mesh.faces[sfi].vertex_indices[..vc] {
            vertex_set.insert(vi);
        }
    }

    let mut face_list: Vec<usize> = seed_faces.to_vec();

    // Fit cylinder to seed faces
    let (mut current_origin, mut current_dir, mut current_radius) = match
        fit_cylinder(&face_list, &vertex_set, &mesh.faces, &mesh.vertices)
    {
        Some(v) => v,
        None => {
            if verbosity >= 3 {
                let seed_str: Vec<String> = seed_faces.iter().map(|f| f.to_string()).collect();
                eprintln!("  [BFS-cyl] seed=({}) fit_cylinder returned None (degenerate geometry)", seed_str.join(","));
            }
            return None;
        }
    };

    // Verify seed: all vertices within tolerance
    if !all_vertices_within_cylinder_tolerance(
        &vertex_set, &current_origin, &current_dir, current_radius,
        vertex_tol, &mesh.vertices,
    ) {
        if verbosity >= 3 {
            let seed_str: Vec<String> = seed_faces.iter().map(|f| f.to_string()).collect();
            let worst_dist = vertex_set.iter().map(|&vi| {
                vertex_to_cylinder_distance(&mesh.vertices[vi], &current_origin, &current_dir, current_radius).abs()
            }).fold(0.0_f64, f64::max);
            eprintln!("  [BFS-cyl] seed=({}) vertex tolerance failed: worst_dist={:.2e} > tol={:.2e}, r={:.6}",
                seed_str.join(","), worst_dist, vertex_tol, current_radius);
        }
        return None;
    }

    // Determine convexity from first seed face
    let convex = determine_convexity(
        &mesh.faces[seed_faces[0]], &mesh.vertices, &current_origin, &current_dir,
    );

    // Verify convexity consistency across all seed faces
    for &sfi in &seed_faces[1..] {
        if determine_convexity(&mesh.faces[sfi], &mesh.vertices, &current_origin, &current_dir) != convex {
            if verbosity >= 3 {
                let seed_str: Vec<String> = seed_faces.iter().map(|f| f.to_string()).collect();
                eprintln!("  [BFS-cyl] seed=({}) convexity inconsistency: face {} is {} but face {} is {}",
                    seed_str.join(","), seed_faces[0], if convex { "convex" } else { "concave" },
                    sfi, if convex { "concave" } else { "convex" });
            }
            return None;
        }
    }

    // Verify all seed face centroids within surface tolerance

                eprintln!("  [BFS-cyl] seed=({}) centroid tolerance failed: face {} cen_dist={:.2e} > tol={:.2e}",
                    seed_str.join(","), sfi, cen_dist, surface_tol);
            }
            return None;
        }
    }

            }).colle

//! Stage 2: Surface Fitting
//!
//! 2.1: Deduce planar hypotheses
//! 2.2: Deduce cylindrical hypotheses
//! 2.3: Deduce spherical hypotheses
//! 2.4: Deduce ruled surface hypotheses
//! 2.5: Deduce NURBS hypotheses
//! 2.6: Select surfaces for reconstruction

use crate::config::Config;
use crate::stage1::{self, ConnectedMesh, MeshFace, MeshVertex, UNDEDUCED_PLANAR_HYPOTHESIS, UNDEDUCED_CYLINDRICAL_HYPOTHESIS, UNDEDUCED_SPHERICAL_HYPOTHESIS, NO_HYPOTHESIS};

/// Lower bound on cross product magnitude |n₁ × n₂| for a valid cylinder/sphere
/// seed pair. Since |n₁ × n₂| = sin(θ) for unit normals, this corresponds to a
/// minimum dihedral angle of arcsin(0.01) ≈ 0.57° (~0.6°). Seed pairs closer
/// than this are too nearly parallel for numerically stable axis estimation
/// (cylinders) or sphere fitting (spheres). Together with `angular_tol` (the
/// upper bound, typically 17.5°), this defines the valid seed-pair window
/// [~0.6°, angular_tol].
const MIN_CROSS_THRESHOLD: f64 = 0.01;

> The current code has a multi-scale grid search that fails to find the exact minimum (gets r=2.073 vs. true r=2.0). The SSE landscape has been confirmed as non-convex for Newton methods on the scalar SSE, but LM works on the *residual vector* and uses trust-region damping, which makes it significantly more robust. 
> 
> Key advantages:
> 1. **LM on residuals is different from Newton on SSE.** The previous Newton attempts failed because they optimized the scalar SSE. LM works with the Jacobian of the vector of residuals, which is much better conditioned.

> 3. **This replaces ~1300 grid search evaluations with just 10-20 LM iterations**, each costing only a handful of residual evaluations — essentially free compared to the grid approach.

use nalgebra::{
    storage::{IsContiguous, RawStorageMut, Storage},
    ComplexField, Dim, Matrix, Vector,
};

/// A least squares minimization problem.
///
/// This is what [`LevenbergMarquardt`](struct.LevenbergMarquardt.html) needs
/// to compute the residuals and the Jacobian. See the [module documentation](index.html)
/// for a usage example.
pub trait LeastSquaresProblem<F, M, N>
where
    F: ComplexField + Copy,
    N: Dim,
    M: Dim,
{
    /// Storage type used for the residuals. Use `nalgebra::storage::Owned<F, M>`
    /// if you want to use `VectorN` or `MatrixMN`.
    type ResidualStorage: RawStorageMut<F, M> + Storage<F, M> + IsContiguous;
    type JacobianStorage: RawStorageMut<F, M, N> + Storage<F, M, N> + IsContiguous;
    type ParameterStorage: RawStorageMut<F, N> + Storage<F, N> + IsContiguous + Clone;

    /// Set the stored parameters `$\vec{x}$`.
    fn set_params(&mut self, x: &Vector<F, N, Self::ParameterStorage>);

    /// Get the current parameter vector `$\vec{x}$`.
    fn params(&self) -> Vector<F, N, Self::ParameterStorage>;

    /// Compute the residual vector.
    fn residuals(&self) -> Option<Vector<F, M, Self::ResidualStorage>>;

    /// Compute the Jacobian of the residual vector.
    fn jacobian(&self) -> Option<Matrix<F, M, N, Self::JacobianStorage>>;
}
```
</details>

        self.params.copy_from(params);

        self.params

        let u1 = Dim::from_usize(1);

/// Compute sum of squared vertex-to-cylinder distances for a given axis direction.
fn cylinder_fit_sse(
    axis_dir: &[f64; 3],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<(f64, [f64; 3], f64)> {
    let (origin, radius) = fit_circle_for_axis(axis_dir, vertex_set, vertices)?;
    let sse: f64 = vertex_set.iter().map(|&vi| {
        let d = vertex_to_cylinder_distance(&vertices[vi], &origin, axis_dir, radius);
        d * d
    }).sum();
    Some((sse, origin, radius))
}

/// Tries two axis estimation strategies — normal-covariance (good for wide arcs) and
/// vertex-PCA (good for narrow arcs / drum-laced triangles) — picks the better one,
/// then refines the axis direction by minimizing vertex-to-cylinder SSE.
/// Returns (axis_origin, axis_direction, radius).
fn fit_cylinder(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Candidate A: Axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    // Works well when face normals span a wide arc of the cylinder.
    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);
    let mut axis_a = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);
    let len_a = normalize3(&mut axis_a);
    let fit_a = if len_a > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_a, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Candidate B: Axis from largest eigenvector of vertex position covariance.
    // Works well for narrow-arc drum-laced triangles where the axial extent
    // exceeds the circumferential arc, making the axis the direction of
    // maximum vertex spread.
    let vert_cov = build_vertex_covariance(vertex_set, vertices);
    let mut axis_b = largest_eigenvector_3x3(&vert_cov);
    let len_b = normalize3(&mut axis_b);
    let fit_b = if len_b > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_b, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Pick the axis candidate with lower SSE.
    let (mut best_sse, mut axis_dir, mut axis_origin, mut radius) = match (fit_a, fit_b) {
        (Some((sse_a, origin_a, r_a)), Some((sse_b, origin_b, r_b))) => {
            if sse_a <= sse_b {
                (sse_a, axis_a, origin_a, r_a)
            } else {
                (sse_b, axis_b, origin_b, r_b)
            }
        }
        (Some((sse, origin, r)), None) => (sse, axis_a, origin, r),
        (None, Some((sse, origin, r))) => (sse, axis_b, origin, r),
        (None, None) => return None,
    };

    // Refine axis direction via multi-scale grid search over tilt angles.
    // The SSE landscape for narrow-arc cylinder fitting is non-convex, with
    // local minima that trap gradient-based methods. Grid search reliably
    // finds the global minimum.
    //
    // Three scales: ±5.7° in 0.57° steps, then ±0.57° in 0.057° steps,
    // then ±0.057° in 0.006° steps. Total ~1300 SSE evaluations, each
    // trivially cheap (2D circle fit on a few vertices).
    for &half_range in &[0.1_f64, 0.01, 0.001] {
        let step_size = half_range / 10.0;
        let (pu, pw) = perpendicular_basis(&axis_dir);
        let base_dir = axis_dir;
        for i in -10_i32..=10 {
            for j in -10_i32..=10 {
                let tu = i as f64 * step_size;
                let tw = j as f64 * step_size;
                let mut trial_dir = [
                    base_dir[0] + tu * pu[0] + tw * pw[0],
                    base_dir[1] + tu * pu[1] + tw * pw[1],
                    base_dir[2] + tu * pu[2] + tw * pw[2],
                ];
                normalize3(&mut trial_dir);
                if let Some((sse, origin, r)) = cylinder_fit_sse(&trial_dir, vertex_set, vertices) {
                    if sse < best_sse {
                        best_sse = sse;
                        axis_dir = trial_dir;
                        axis_origin = origin;
                        radius = r;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    Some((axis_origin, axis_dir, radius))
}

/// Check if all vertices in a set are within tolerance of a cylinder surface.

    axis_direction: &[f64; 3],

    tolerance: f64,
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> bool {

        let d = vertex_to_cylinder_distance(&vertices[vi], axis_origin, axis_direction, radius);

```
fn fit_cylinder(
    face_indices: &[usize],
    vertex_set: &HashSet<usize>,
    faces: &[MeshFace],
    vertices: &[MeshVertex],
) -> Option<([f64; 3], [f64; 3], f64)> {
    // Step 1: Axis direction from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    let cov = build_normal_covariance(face_indices, faces, vertices);
    let mut axis_dir = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);
    let len = normalize3(&mut axis_dir);
    if len < 1e-15 {
        return None;
    }

    // Step 2: Compute orthogonal basis perpendicular to axis.
    let (u, w) = perpendicular_basis(&axis_dir);

    // Step 3: Project vertices onto 2D plane perpendicular to axis and fit circle.
    let verts: Vec<usize> = vertex_set.iter().copied().collect();
    let n = verts.len();
    if n < 3 {
        return None;
    }

    // Collect 2D coordinates
    let mut xs = Vec::with_capacity(n);
    let mut ys = Vec::with_capacity(n);
    for &vi in &verts {
        let v = &vertices[vi];
        let vx = v.x;
        let vy = v.y;
        let vz = v.z;
        xs.push(vx * u[0] + vy * u[1] + vz * u[2]);
        ys.push(vx * w[0] + vy * w[1] + vz * w[2]);
    }

    // Center the 2D data to improve numerical conditioning.
    // Without centering, the normal equations matrix becomes ill-conditioned
    // when absolute coordinates are large relative to the arc span (e.g.,
    // 6 vertices spanning 2.5° of a r=2mm cylinder at position (15, 12.5)).
    let nf = n as f64;
    let mean_x = xs.iter().sum::<f64>() / nf;
    let mean_y = ys.iter().sum::<f64>() / nf;
    for i in 0..n {
        xs[i] -= mean_x;
        ys[i] -= mean_y;
    }

    // Algebraic circle fit: x² + y² + Dx + Ey + F = 0
    // Least squares: minimize Σ(x² + y² + Dx + Ey + F)²
    // Normal equations: [Σx²  Σxy  Σx ] [D]   [-Σx(x²+y²)]
```
</details>

> 💭 The old code used ONLY the normal covariance approach (smallest eigenvector). No dual-axis, no vertex PCA candidate. And no grid search refinement either. The old code just directly fits with the normal covariance axis.
> 
> So the difference is that the old code used the normal-covariance axis directly, while my code adds:1. A vertex-PCA alternate candidate2. Picks the lower-SSE candidate between the two  3. Runs LM refinementThe issue is that for seed (3,69,68) on the plate_with_hole, the vertex-PCA axis (candidate B) has a lower SSE than the normal-covariance axis (candidate A), even though candidate B's axis is completely wrong. 
> 
> I'm realizing the problem might be that when you project vertices onto a completely wrong axis, the resulting circle fit can still appear to have a small radius if the 3D arrangement of points happens to align in a way that creates a plausible projection. The real issue is that the old code avoided this entirely by only using the normal covariance axis, whereas my dual-candidate approach is picking the wrong one based on SSE alone.
> 
> Looking at the specifics: for seed (3,69,68), the normal covariance gives the correct axis with radius 5.000122, but my new code is selecting the vertex-PCA candidate instead because it has lower SSE—even though that axis is fundamentally wrong. The root cause is that vertex PCA fails for thin cylinders where the vertex spread along the true axis is small; the largest eigenvector ends up pointing radially instead of axially, producing a garbage estimate.
> 
> I need to either drop the vertex-PCA candidate entirely or add a condition to only use it when the normal covariance axis is already unreliable.
> 
> Actually, I'm realizing the real issue: the SSE comparison itself is flawed. When the vertex-PCA axis is perpendicular to the true axis, the vertices project onto a near-line pattern, and the circle-fitting algorithm gives an artificially low SSE because it's fitting a circle to points that are essentially collinear. So the comparison is picking the wrong candidate based on a misleading metric. small radius, and the sum of squared distances happens to be small — the small circle sits very close to all the vertices because they're all basically on a line "through" the circle.
> 
> Wait, no. If the vertices are on a line in the projected plane, the algebraic circle fit should give a very large radius (a circle degenerating into a line), not a small radius. But the algebraic fit doesn't minimize geometric distance — it minimizes algebraic distance, which can give surprising results for near-degenerate configurations.
> 
> Actually, the algebraic circle fit for points on a line: the equation x² + y² + Dx + Ey + F = 0. If all points are on a line (say along the x-axis, y=0), we get x² + Dx + F = 0 and the matrix becomes rank-deficient. The fit is degenerate and could return anything. With a tiny y-spread, we get a tiny circle that barely fits through the points by cutting across the line. So the real fix is to run Levenberg-Marquardt optimization from both candidate circles and pick whichever one gives the lower final sum of squared errors.

I see. The old code only used normal covariance (no vertex-PCA candidate), and the vertex-PCA axis is winning the SSE comparison even though it's wrong. The fix is to run LM from BOTH starting points and pick the better result:

<details>
<summary>Reading lines 1270-1320 of <a href="../src/stage2.rs#L1270-L1321">stage2.rs</a></summary>

```
    let mut axis_b = largest_eigenvector_3x3(&vert_cov);
    let len_b = normalize3(&mut axis_b);
    let fit_b = if len_b > 1e-15 {
        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_b, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {
        None
    };

    // Pick the axis candidate with lower SSE.
    let (axis_dir, axis_origin, radius) = match (fit_a, fit_b) {
        (Some((sse_a, origin_a, r_a)), Some((sse_b, origin_b, r_b))) => {
            if sse_a <= sse_b {
                (axis_a, origin_a, r_a)
            } else {
                (axis_b, origin_b, r_b)
            }
        }
        (Some((_sse, origin, r)), None) => (axis_a, origin, r),
        (None, Some((_sse, origin, r))) => (axis_b, origin, r),
        (None, None) => return None,
    };

    // Refine with Levenberg-Marquardt optimization on radial residuals.
    let points: Vec<[f64; 3]> = vertex_set.iter()
        .map(|&vi| [vertices[vi].x, vertices[vi].y, vertices[vi].z])
        .collect();
    let problem = CylinderLMProblem::new(points, axis_dir, axis_origin, radius);
    let (result, report) = LevenbergMarquardt::new().minimize(problem);
    let refined_dir = result.axis_dir_from_params(&result.params);
    let refined_origin = [result.params[2], result.params[3], result.params[4]];
    let refined_radius = result.params[5];

    let fi_str: Vec<String> = face_indices.iter().map(|f| f.to_string()).collect();
    eprintln!("LM faces=({}): initial dir=[{:.6},{:.6},{:.6}] origin=[{:.4},{:.4},{:.4}] r={:.6}",
        fi_str.join(","), axis_dir[0], axis_dir[1], axis_dir[2], axis_origin[0], axis_origin[1], axis_origin[2], radius);
    eprintln!("LM faces=({}): refined dir=[{:.6},{:.6},{:.6}] origin=[{:.4},{:.4},{:.4}] r={:.6} term={:?}",
        fi_str.join(","), refined_dir[0], refined_dir[1], refined_dir[2], refined_origin[0], refined_origin[1], refined_origin[2],
        refined_radius, report.termination);

    if refined_radius > 0.0 {
        Some((refined_origin, refined_dir, refined_radius))
    } else {
        Some((axis_origin, axis_dir, radius))
    }
}

    if vertex_set.len() < 3 {

    // Candidate A: Axis from smallest eigenvector of normal covariance.
    // Works well when face normals span a wide arc of the cylinder.

    let mut axis_a = smallest_eigenvector_3x3(&cov);

        cylinder_fit_sse(&axis_a, vertex_set, vertices)
    } else {

    // Candidate B: Axis from largest eigenvector of vertex position covariance.
    // Works well for narrow-arc drum-laced triangles where the axial extent
    // exceeds the circumferential arc, making the axis the direction of
    // maximum vertex spread.
    let vert_cov = build_vertex_covariance(vertex_set, vertices);
    let mut axis_b = largest_eigenvector_3x3(&vert_cov);
    let len_b = normalize3(&mut axis_b);
    let fit_b = if len_b > 1e-15 {

    } else {
